P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q\equiv_{FT}^{*}P^{\prime}\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q^{\prime}
.
This is equivalent to showing that
FT^{*}(P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q)
is completely determined by
FT^{*}(P)
,
S

and
FT^{*}(Q)
.

Below I define, for each
S\subseteq A
, a binary operator
\mathbin{\|_{S}}
that takes as arguments
sets of partial failure traces, and produces again
a set of partial failure traces. In the appendix—Proposition
—I prove that

FT^{*}(P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q)=\textit{col}(FT^{*}(P)\mathbin{\|_{S}}FT^{*}(Q))\;.

(4)

This yields Theorem
.

In order to define the operator
\mathbin{\|_{S}}
, I analyse how a partial failure trace of
P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q
decomposes into partial failure
traces of
P
and
Q
.

Definition 6.4
.

decomposition
A
decomposition
of a partial failure trace

\sigma\mathbin{=}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{n}\top\mathbin{\in}(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top

w.r.t. a set
S\mathbin{\subseteq}A
is a pair
\sigma_{\rm L},\sigma_{\rm R}
of partial failure
traces, obtained by leaving out all
\star
-elements from sequences

\sigma^{\rm L}=\sigma^{\rm L}_{1}\sigma^{\rm L}_{2}\cdots\sigma^{\rm L}_{n}\top

and

\sigma^{\rm R}=\sigma^{\rm R}_{1}\sigma^{\rm R}_{2}\cdots\sigma^{\rm R}_{n}\top\mathbin{\in}(A\cup\{\star\}\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top
,
such that, for all
i=1,\dots,n
,

•

if
\sigma_{i}\in A\setminus S
then either
\sigma^{\rm L}_{i}=\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma^{\rm R}_{i}=\star
,
or
\sigma^{\rm R}_{i}=\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma^{\rm L}_{i}=\star
,

•

if
\sigma_{i}\in S
then
\sigma^{\rm L}_{i}=\sigma^{\rm R}_{i}=\sigma_{i}
, and

•

if
\sigma_{i}\in\Pow(A)
then
\sigma^{\rm L}_{i}\in\Pow(A)
and
\sigma^{\rm R}_{i}\in\Pow(A)
.

This decomposition is
valid
if, for each
i=1,\dots,n
for which
\sigma_{i}\in\Pow(A)
, one has

\displaystyle\hskip-9.0pt\mbox{$\sigma_{i}$ is system-ended in $\sigma$}\Leftrightarrow\big((\mbox{$\sigma_{i}^{\rm L}$ is system-ended in $\sigma_{\rm L}$})\!\vee\!(\mbox{$\sigma_{i}^{\rm R}$ is system-ended in $\sigma_{\rm R}$})\big)\!\!\!

(5)

\displaystyle\neg\big((\mbox{$\sigma_{i}^{\rm L}$ is system-ended in $\sigma_{\rm L}$})\!\wedge\!(\mbox{$\sigma_{i}^{\rm R}$ is system-ended in $\sigma_{\rm R}$})\big)\!\!\!

(6)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{\rm L}\setminus S=\sigma_{i}\setminus S=\sigma_{i}^{\rm R}\setminus S\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\sigma_{i}^{\rm L}\cap S)\cup(\sigma_{i}^{\rm R}\cap S)=\sigma_{i}\cap S\;.

(7)

For
F,G\subseteq(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top
sets of partial failure traces, let

F\mathbin{\|_{S}}G\subseteq(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top
be the set of partial failure traces

\sigma
, such that for some valid decomposition one has
\sigma_{\rm L}\in F
and
\sigma_{\rm R}\in G
.

Definition
says that in any partial failure trace
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q)
, each occurrence of an action
a\notin S
in

\sigma
must stem from either
P
or
Q
, whereas each occurrence of an action
a\in S
must stem
from both. A set
X
occurring in
\sigma
denotes a period of idling; necessarily both
P
and
Q

idle during this period.
I justify (
5
)–(
7
) informally below; formally, these requirements
are fully justified by the fact that they allow me to prove (
4
).

In a parallel composition
P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q
there are three parties that can block actions, or end idle periods:
P
,
Q

and the global environment
\mathcal{E}
of the composition. The environment of
P
consists of
Q
and
\mathcal{E}
.

An idle period of
P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q
ends through a single time-out. If this timeout occurs
in
P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q
itself, rather then in its environment, it must occur in exactly one of the components
P
and
Q
.
And if the time-out stems from the environment it occurs in neither component.
This explains conditions (
5
) and (
6
).

An action
a\notin S
can, from the perspective of one component, not be
blocked by the other component. So it is blocked by the environment of that component iff it is
blocked by the environment
\mathcal{E}
of the parallel composition. Hence

a\in\sigma_{i}^{\rm L}\Leftrightarrow a\in\sigma_{i}\Leftrightarrow a\in\sigma_{i}^{\rm R}
.
This gives the first formula of (
7
).

An action
a\in S
requires cooperation of all three parties,
P
,
Q
and
\mathcal{E}
,
so it can be blocked by any of them.
If such an action is in
\sigma_{i}^{\rm L}
(resp.
\sigma_{i}^{\rm R}
), it is not blocked by the environment of
P

(resp.
Q
), and thus certainly not by
\mathcal{E}
.
This gives direction
\subseteq
of the second formula of (
7
).

To justify the other direction, note that each set
X
occurring in a partial failure trace of a
process
R
corresponds with path
R_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}R_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.10275pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}R_{n}

(its realm),
where
R_{0}
is reachable from
R
, such that (i) all
\alpha_{j}
are either
\tau
or
{\rm t}
, and (ii)
for each
j\in\{0,\dots,n{-}1\}
such that
\alpha_{j+1}={\rm t}
, and also for
j=n
, one has
R_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta$\>}\hfil}}$}}

for all
\beta\in X\cup\{\tau\}
. In the special case that
R=P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q
, and where
X(=\sigma_{i})
is
decomposed into
X_{\rm L}(=\sigma_{i}^{\rm L})
and
X_{\rm R}(=\sigma_{i}^{\rm R})
, each such
R_{j}
has the
form
P_{j}\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q_{j}
, with
P_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta$\>}\hfil}}$}}
for
\beta\in X_{\rm L}\cup\{\tau\}
and

Q_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta$\>}\hfil}}$}}
for
\beta\in X_{\rm R}\cup\{\tau\}
. Since, for
a\in S
,
P_{j}\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle a$\>}\hfil}}$}}

iff either
P_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle a$\>}\hfil}}$}}
or
Q_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle a$\>}\hfil}}$}}
, one can always choose the decomposition of
X
into

X_{\rm L}
and
X_{\rm R}
in such a way that
a\in X
implies
a\in X_{\rm L}\vee a\in X_{\rm R}
.
This gives direction
\supseteq
of the second formula of (
7
).

There is one circumstance, however, where the above argument breaks down, namely if different
choices of
j\in\{0,\dots,n\}
give rise to a different decomposition of
X
into
X_{\rm L}
and
X_{\rm R}
.
However, in such a case one can simply write the occurrence of
X
as
XX\dots X
, such that now
each occurrence of
X
has a unique decomposition. The original failure trace is then recovered by
the closure operator
col
in (
4
).

The following example shows the necessity of Condition (
5
).

Example 6.5
.

consistency
Consider the process
a\mathbin{\|_{\emptyset}}{\rm t}.b
.
Here
\{b\}a\top\in FT^{*}(a)
and
\{b\}b\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.b)
.
So without Condition (
5
) one would obtain
\{b\}ab\top\in FT^{*}(a\mathbin{\|_{\emptyset}}{\rm t}.b)
.
Yet
a\mathbin{\|_{\emptyset}}{\rm t}.b
has no such partial failure trace.

Two similar examples, without and with synchronisation, show the necessity of Condition (
6
).

Example 6.6
.

consistency2
Consider the process
{\rm t}.a\mathbin{\|_{\emptyset}}{\rm t}.b
.
Here
\{a,b\}a\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.a)
and
\{a,b\}b\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.b)
.
So without Condition (
6
) one would obtain
\{a,b\}ab\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.a\mathbin{\|_{\emptyset}}{\rm t}.b)
.
Yet
{\rm t}.a\mathbin{\|_{\emptyset}}{\rm t}.b
has no such partial failure trace.

Example 6.7
.

consistency2S
Consider the process
{\rm t}.(\tau+b)\mathbin{\|_{\{b\}}}{\rm t}.(\tau+b)
.
Here
\{b\}b\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.(\tau+b))
.
So without Condition (
6
) one would obtain
\{b\}b\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.(\tau+b)\mathbin{\|_{\{b\}}}{\rm t}.(\tau+b))
.
Yet, as explained at (
2
),
{\rm t}.(\tau+b)\mathbin{\|_{\{b\}}}{\rm t}.(\tau+b)
has no such partial failure trace.

The next example shows that the operation
col
in (
4
) cannot be omitted.

Example 6.8
.

collapse required
One has
\sigma:=\{b\}\{b\}a\top\in FT^{*}(b+{\rm t}.a)\mathbin{\|_{\{a,b\}}}FT^{*}({\rm t}.(b+{\rm t}.a))
, taking

\sigma_{\rm L}:=\emptyset\{b\}a\top
and
\sigma_{\rm R}:=\{b\}\emptyset a\top
. Namely
\top\in FT^{*}(0)
,
a\top\in FT^{*}(a)
,

\{b\}a\top\in FT^{*}(a)
,
\emptyset\{b\}a\top\in FT^{*}(a)
, so
\emptyset\{b\}a\top\in FT^{*}(b+{\rm t}.a)
,
and
\emptyset a\top\in FT^{*}(a)
,
\emptyset a\top\in FT^{*}(b+{\rm t}.a)
,

\emptyset a\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.(b+{\rm t}.a))
,
so

\{b\}\emptyset a\top\in FT^{*}({\rm t}.(b+{\rm t}.a))
.

Yet
\{b\}a\top\notin FT^{*}(b+{\rm t}.a)\mathbin{\|_{\{a,b\}}}FT^{*}({\rm t}.(b+{\rm t}.a))
.

Theorem 6.9
.

congruence abstraction

\equiv_{FT}^{*}
is a congruence for the abstraction operators
\tau_{I}
.

Proof 6.10
.

A partial failure trace
\rho

survives abstraction from

I\subseteq A
iff

(1)

each set
X
occurring in
\rho
satisfies
I\subseteq X
,

(2)

each subsequence
Xc_{0}\dots c_{n}Y
with
c_{0},\dots,c_{n}\in I
satisfies
X=Y
, and

(3)

each subsequence
Xc_{0}\dots c_{n}a
with
c_{0},\dots,c_{n}\in I
and
a\notin I
satisfies
a\in X
.

If this is the case, then
\tau_{I}(\rho)
is the result of contracting any subsequence
Xc_{0}\dots c_{n}X

of
\rho
to
X
, and omitting all remaining occurrences of actions
c\in I
.
If this is not the case
\tau_{I}(\rho)
is undefined.
Let
\sigma\cup I
denote the partial failure trace obtained from a partial failure trace

\sigma
by replacing each occurrence of
X
in
\sigma
by
X\cup I
. I claim that

\sigma\in FT^{*}(\tau_{I}(P))~~\Leftrightarrow~~\exists\rho\in FT^{*}(P).~\tau_{I}(\rho)=\sigma\cup I\;.

(8)

This shows that
FT^{*}(\tau_{I}(P))
is completely determined by
FT^{*}(P)
and
I
,
which yields Theorem
.
A formal proof of (
8
) is given in the appendix—Proposition
.

Intuitively, an occurrence of
X
in
\sigma
denotes a period of idling in which
X
is the set of
actions allowed by the environment
\mathcal{E}
of
\tau_{I}(P)
. The environment
\mathcal{E}_{P}
of

P
when
P
is placed in a
\tau_{I}(\_\!\_)
-context always allows the actions from
I
, and for the
rest is like
\mathcal{E}
. This explains (i) and the use of
\sigma\cup I
in (
8
).
Conditions (ii) and (iii) express that the instantaneous occurrence of a sequence of internal actions does
not constitute an opportune moment for the environment to change its mind on which actions are allowed.
Since
c_{0}\in X
(by (i)), the period
X
of idling ends through a time-out action of the system, right
before the occurrence of
c_{0}
. Whereas in
P
the action
c_{0}
could synchronise with the
environment, and thus cause a change in the set of allowed actions, this option is no longer
available for
\tau_{I}(P)
. So after the sequence of internal actions, the same set of actions
X
is
allowed by the environment, either resulting in further idling, or in the execution of an action
a\in X
.

An occurrence of
X
in
\sigma
imposes the requirement on some reachable states
\tau_{I}(P^{\prime})

of
\tau_{I}(P)
that
\tau_{I}(P^{\prime})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\}
. It is
satisfied iff
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup I\cup\{\tau\}
.
The particular way of hiding actions
c\in I
in
\tau_{I}(\rho)
, together with Conditions (ii) and
(iii), exactly matches the two rules for bridging a time-out transition in Table
3
.

Theorem 6.11
.

congruence renaming

\equiv_{FT}^{*}
is a congruence for the relational renaming operators
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof 6.12
.

For
X\subseteq A
, let
\mathcal{R}^{-1}(X):=\{a\in A\mid\exists b\in X.~(a,b)\in\mathcal{R}\}
.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\sigma)
denote the set of partial failure traces obtained from
\sigma

by (i) replacing each occurrence of a set
X
by the set
\mathcal{R}^{-1}(X)
, and
(ii) replacing each occurrence of an action
b
by some action
a
with
(a,b)\in\mathcal{R}
,
subject to the condition that if the occurrence of
b
is preceded by an occurrence of a set
X
,
then
b\in X
iff
a\in\mathcal{R}^{-1}(X)
. Now, as shown by Proposition
in the appendix,

\sigma\in FT^{*}(\mathcal{R}(P))~~\Leftrightarrow~~\exists\rho\in FT^{*}(P).~\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\sigma)\;.

(9)

The side condition ensures that for each period of idling, the party that ends it (system or
environment) does not differ between
\sigma
and
\rho
.
This shows that
FT^{*}(\mathcal{R}(P))
is completely determined by
\mathcal{R}
and
P
, which yields Theorem
.

The following example shows that the side condition in the definition of
\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\sigma)
cannot be omitted.

Example 6.13
.

renaming
Let
P={\rm t}.a
and
\mathcal{R}=\{(a,b),(a,c)\}
. Then
\rho=\{a\}a\top\in FT^{*}(P)
, so without the side
condition one would obtain
\sigma=\{b\}c\top\in FT^{*}(\mathcal{R}(P))
. However,
\mathcal{R}(P)
has no such failure
trace, for if the environment allows just
b
when the time-out occurs,
\mathcal{R}(P)
will proceed with

b
rather than
c
.

Rooted partial failure trace semantics

Partial failure trace equivalence, however, like all default equivalences that abstract from
internal activity
[
Mil90
]
,
fails to be a congruence for the choice operator
+
. In particular,
FT^{*}(b)=FT^{*}(\tau.b)
, yet

FT^{*}(a+b)\neq FT^{*}(a+\tau.b)
, for only
a+\tau.b
has a partial failure trace
\{a\}\top
, and
only
a+b
has a partial failure trace
\emptyset a\top
.
The classical way to solve this problem for languages like CCSP is to add one bit of information to
the semantics of processes. Two CCSP processes could be called
partial failure trace congruent

iff
FT^{*}(P)=FT^{*}(Q)\wedge\textit{stable}(P)\Leftrightarrow\textit{stable}(Q)
.
This turns out to be a congruence for CCSP, and moreover the coarsest congruence finer than partial failure trace equivalence.
In the presence of time-outs this one-bit modification of partial failure trace equivalence is insufficient,
for
FT^{*}({\rm t}.b)=FT^{*}({\rm t}.{\rm t}.b)
, yet
FT^{*}(a+{\rm t}.b)\neq FT^{*}(a+{\rm t}.{\rm t}.b)
. Namely, only
a+{\rm t}.{\rm t}.b

has a partial failure trace
\{b\}\{a,b\}b
.

To make partial failure trace equivalence a congruence, one needs additionally to keep track of
partial failure traces of the form
X\sigma
that are lifted by the fifth rule of Table
3

through an initial
{\rm t}
-transition. I also use an additional bit, telling that a stable state
is reachable along a nonempty path of
\tau
-transitions.

Definition 6.14
.

rfft
Let
FT^{r*}(P):=

FT^{*}(P)\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{}\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\mid P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}}\}\cup\{{\rm t}X\sigma\mid\exists P^{\prime}.~P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}\wedge X\sigma\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})\wedge P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}}\wedge\mathcal{I}(P)\cap X=\emptyset\}\\
\mbox{}\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\mid P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}}\wedge\emptyset\top\in FT^{*}(P)\}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Processes
P,Q\in\mbox{\bbb P}
are
rooted partial failure trace equivalent
,
P\equiv_{FT}^{r*}Q
, iff
FT^{r*}(P)=FT^{r*}(Q)
.

One has
{\rm t}.b\not\equiv_{FT}^{r*}{\rm t}.{\rm t}.b
because
{\rm t}\{a,b\}b\in FT^{r*}({\rm t}.{\rm t}.b)
but
{\rm t}\{a,b\}b\notin FT^{r*}({\rm t}.b)
.

For the purpose of defining
\equiv_{FT}^{r*}
, the
poststab
bit is redundant,
as it is clearly determined by the other elements of
FT^{r*}(P)
.
However, it will turn out to be useful in defining a rooted partial failure trace preorder

\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}
in Section
7
.

{obs}

Rooted partial failure trace equivalence is finer than partial failure trace equivalence.

Let
\textit{ini}_{Z}(F)
, for
Z\mathbin{\subseteq}A
and
F\mathbin{\subseteq}(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top
, be the least set such that
(a)
F\mathbin{\subseteq}\textit{ini}_{Z}(F)
, (b)
\top\mathbin{\in}\textit{ini}_{Z}(F)
, and (c) if
\sigma\in\textit{ini}_{Z}(F)
and
X\cap Z=\emptyset
then

X\sigma\in\textit{ini}_{Z}(F)
. It represents the set of partial failure traces of a stable process
P

derivable by the first and fourth rule of Table
3
when
F\mathbin{\subseteq}FT^{*}(P)
and
\mathcal{I}(P)\mathbin{=}Z
.

Theorem 6.15
.

congruence
Rooted partial failure trace equivalence (
\equiv_{FT}^{r*}
) is a congruence for the operators of CCSP
t
.

Proof 6.16
.

It suffices to show that
FT^{r*}(P+Q)
is fully determined by
FT^{r*}(P)
and
FT^{r*}(Q)
, that

FT^{r*}(\alpha.P)
is fully determined by
\alpha
and
FT^{r*}(P)
, and similarly for
FT^{r*}(P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q)
,
FT^{r*}(\tau_{I}(P))
and
FT^{r*}(\mathcal{R}(P))
.
The following equations establish this.

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(a.P)~~=~~

\displaystyle\textit{ini}_{\{a\}}\big(\{a\sigma\mid\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)\}\big)\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\}

(10)

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(\tau.P)~~=~~

\displaystyle FT^{*}(P)\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\mid\emptyset\top\in FT^{*}(P)\}

(11)

\displaystyle FT^{r*}({\rm t}.P)~~=~~

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textit{ini}_{\emptyset}\big(\{X\sigma\mid X\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)\}\cup\{Xa\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)\wedge a\in X\}\big)\\
\mbox{}\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\}\cup\{{\rm t}X\sigma\mid X\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)\}\end{array}

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(P+Q)~~=~~

\displaystyle\!\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}ll@{}}FT^{r*}(P)\cup FT^{r*}(Q)&\mbox{if}~~\neg{\it stable}(P)\wedge\neg{\it stable}(Q)\\
\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\}\cup FT^{r*}(Q)&\mbox{if}~~{\it stable}(P)\wedge\neg{\it stable}(Q)\\
FT^{r*}(P)\cup\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{r*}(Q)\}&\mbox{if}~~\neg{\it stable}(P)\wedge{\it stable}(Q)\\
\textit{ini}_{\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\mathcal{I}(Q)}\big(H\big)\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\}\cup K&\mbox{if}~~{\it stable}(P)\wedge{\it stable}(Q)\\
\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(P\mathbin{\|_{S}}Q)~~=~~

\displaystyle\textit{col}(FT^{r*}(P)\mathbin{\|_{S}}FT^{r*}(Q))

(18)

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(\tau_{I}(P))~~=~~

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}ll@{}}\{\sigma\mid(\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\neq\sigma\neq\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab})\wedge\exists\rho\in FT^{r*}(P).~\tau_{I}(\rho)=\sigma\cup I\}\\
\mbox{}\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\mid\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\in FT^{r*}(P)\wedge\mathcal{I}(P)\cap I=\emptyset\}\\
\mbox{}\cup\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\mid\begin{array}[t]{@{}ll@{}}(\exists c_{0}c_{1}\dots c_{n}I\top\in FT^{r*}(P)\mbox{~where~}n\geq 0\mbox{~and all~}c_{i}\in I)\\
\mbox{}\vee(\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in FT^{r*}(P)\wedge I\top\in FT^{r*}(P))\}\end{array}\end{array}

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(\mathcal{R}(P))~~=~~

\displaystyle\{\sigma\mid\exists\rho\in FT^{r*}(P).~\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\sigma)\}

(23)

Here
H:=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\cup FT^{r*}(Q)\}\cup\mbox{}\\
\{X\sigma\mid{\rm t}X\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\cup FT^{r*}(Q)\wedge(\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\mathcal{I}(Q))\cap X=\emptyset\}\cup\mbox{}\\
\{Xa\sigma\mid Xa\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\cup FT^{r*}(Q)\wedge a\mathbin{\in}X\wedge(\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\mathcal{I}(Q))\cap X=\emptyset\}\end{array}

and
K=\{{\rm t}X\sigma\mid{\rm t}X\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\cup FT^{r*}(Q)\wedge(\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\mathcal{I}(Q))\cap X=\emptyset\}
.

The first four equations follow immediately from Definition
and Table
3
.
In (
6.16
) the argument of
\textit{ini}_{\emptyset}
generates the partial failure traces
contributed by the fifth and sixth rule of Table
3
; the operation
\textit{ini}_{\emptyset}
then closes
this set under applications of the first and fourth rule.
In the fourth case of (
6.16
), the three lines of
H
generate the partial failure traces
contributed by the second, fifth and sixth rule of Table
3
, respectively; the operation

\textit{ini}_{\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\mathcal{I}(Q)}
then closes this set under applications of the first and fourth rule.

To see that (
10
)–(
6.16
) fulfil their intended purpose, note that
FT^{*}(P)
is fully determined by
FT^{r*}\!(P)
,
namely by dropping the bits
stab
and
poststab
, and all traces
{\rm t}\sigma
.
Moreover
{\it stable}(P)
holds iff
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\mathbin{\in}FT^{r*}(P)
and for stable
P
the set
\mathcal{I}(P)
can be computed
by
\mathcal{I}(P)=\{a\mathbin{\in}A\mid\exists\sigma.~a\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{r*}(P)\}
.

To explain (
18
), the definition of decomposition needs to be extended to rooted partial
failure traces
{\rm t}X\sigma
. In the first bullet-point of Definition
,
“
\sigma_{i}\in A\setminus S
” should now be read as “
\sigma_{i}\in(A\setminus S)\cup\{{\rm t}\}
”.
The definition of
F\mathbin{\|_{S}}G
is unchanged, except that rooted
partial failure traces
{\rm t}X\sigma
are dropped from
F\mathbin{\|_{S}}G
unless
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\in F
and
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\in G
;
furthermore
stab
is deemed to be in
F\mathbin{\|_{S}}G
iff it is in both
F
and
G
, and

poststab
is deemed to be in
F\mathbin{\|_{S}}G
if either
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in F\wedge\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in G
, or

\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in F\wedge\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\in G
, or
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\in F\wedge\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in G
.
Moreover, the operator
col
extends to sets of rooted partial failure traces.
In the appendix it is shown that with these definitions (
18
) holds—see Proposition
.

In (
6.16
), the concept “survives abstraction from
I\subseteq A
” from the proof of
Theorem
is applied verbatim to rooted partial failure traces
{\rm t}X\sigma
,
as is the operation
\tau_{I}
. The validity of (
6.16
) is shown in the
appendix—Proposition
.

In (
23
), the operator
\mathcal{R}^{-1}
extends verbatim to rooted partial failure traces.
The validity of (
6.16
) is shown in the appendix—Proposition
.

As remarked in
[
Gla94
]
, the countable choice operator
\sum_{i\in\mbox{\bbbs N}}P_{i}
is expressible in terms
of the binary choice operator
+
and recursion:

\sum_{i\in\mbox{\bbbs N}}P_{i}=\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$/$}}}{{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[1.29167pt]{\tiny$\backslash$}}}\!\!X_{0}|\mathcal{S}\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\backslash$}}}{{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[1.29167pt]{\tiny$/$}}}$}
, where
X_{i}=P_{i}+X_{i+1}
for
i\in\mbox{\bbb N}
.
An equation similar to (
6.16
) shows that
\equiv_{FT}^{r*}
also is a congruence for the countable choice.

Theorem 6.17
.

full abstraction +
Rooted partial failure trace equivalence (
\equiv_{FT}^{r*}
) is the largest congruence for the operators of CCSP
t

that is included in
\equiv_{FT}^{*}
.

Proof 6.18
.

In view of Observation
6
and Theorem
it suffices to find, for any two
rooted partial failure trace inequivalent processes
P\not\equiv_{FT}^{r*}Q
, a CCSP
t
-context

\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{C}(P)\not\equiv_{FT}^{*}\mathcal{C}(Q)
.

First of all, one can apply a bijective renaming operator on
P
and
Q
, mapping
A
to
A\setminus\{f\}

for some action
f\mathbin{\in}A
. Consequently, I may assume that
f
is
fresh
in the sense that
for each process
R
reachable from
P
or
Q
one has
R{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle f$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
.
For any such
R
one has

\sigma X\rho\in FT^{*}(R)\Leftrightarrow\sigma(X{\cup}\{f\})\rho\in FT^{*}(R).

Let
\sigma\in FT^{r*}(Q)\setminus FT^{r*}(P)
. (The other case proceeds by symmetry.)
The only relevant cases are that
\sigma\mathbin{=}\mbox{\scriptsize\sc(post)}\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}
or
\sigma\mathbin{=}{\rm t}X\rho
,
since in all other cases one immediately has
\sigma\in FT^{*}(Q)\setminus FT^{*}(P)
.

Let
\sigma=\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}
, that is,
Q
is stable but
P
is not. Then
\emptyset f\top\in FT^{*}(Q+f)
but

\emptyset f\top\notin FT^{*}(P+f)
. So it suffices to take the context
\mathcal{C}(\_\!\_\,)=\_\!\_\,+f
.

Now let
\sigma\mathbin{=}{\rm t}X\rho
. So
Q{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}

for all
\alpha\mathbin{\in}X\cup\{\tau\}
,
Q\mathbin{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}}Q^{\prime}
and
X\rho\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(Q^{\prime})
.
I may assume that
P
is stable, as the case that
Q
is stable but
P
is not has been treated already.
By Observation
5
one has
XX\rho\in FT^{*}(Q^{\prime})
, and (*) yields
X^{-}X^{+}\rho\in FT^{*}(Q^{\prime})
, where

X^{-}:=X\setminus\{f\}
and
X^{+}:=X\cup\{f\}
.
The fifth rule of Table
3
gives
X^{-}X^{+}\rho\in FT^{*}(Q{+}f)
, using that
Q{+}f{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}

for all
\alpha\in X^{-}\cup\{\tau\}
.
It suffices to show that
X^{-}X^{+}\rho\notin FT^{*}(P{+}f)
, for then the context

\mathcal{C}(\_\!\_\,)=\_\!\_\,+f
finishes the proof.

So assume, towards a contradiction, that
X^{-}X^{+}\rho\in FT^{*}(P{+}f)
.
This could not have been derived by the fourth rule of Table
3
, since surely

X^{+}\rho\notin FT^{*}(P{+}f)
. Hence the fifth rule must have been used, so that

P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\mathbin{\in}X\cup\{\tau\}
,

P\mathbin{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}}P^{\prime}
and
X^{-}X^{+}\rho\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
.
Now
XX\rho\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
by (*), and
X\rho\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
by Observation
5
.
Thus
{\rm t}X\rho\mathbin{\in}FT^{r*}(P)
, yielding the required contradiction.

Finally, let
\sigma\mathbin{=}\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}
. Then
\{f\}\top\in FT^{*}(Q+f)
, yet
\{f\}\top\notin FT^{*}(P+f)
.

In the quest for a congruence, an alternative solution to changing partial failure trace equivalence
into rooted partial failure trace equivalence, is to restrict the expressiveness of CCSP
t
.
Let CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
be the version of CCSP
t
where the binary operator
+
is replaced by guarded
sums
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}
, for
n\mathbin{\in}\mbox{\bbb N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Here each guarded sum
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}.\_\!\_\,
counts as a separate
n
-ary operator.
The constant
0
and action prefixing
\alpha.P
are the special cases with
n=0
and
n=1
.
The congruence property for guarded sums demands
that
P_{i}\equiv_{FT}^{*}Q_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
implies
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}
.
That this holds is an immediately consequence of Theorem
below.

7.
Partial failure trace preorders

Here I introduce (rooted) partial failure trace preorders
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
and

\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}
in such a way that (rooted) partial failure trace equivalence is its
kernel, i.e.,
P\equiv_{FT}^{*}Q\Leftrightarrow(P\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q\wedge Q\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}P)

and, likewise,
P\equiv_{FT}^{r*}Q\Leftrightarrow(P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q\wedge Q\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}P)
.
These preorders should be defined in such a way that they are precongruences.

Definition 7.1
.

fftpre
Write
P\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q
, for processes
P,Q\mathbin{\in}\mbox{\bbb P}
, iff
FT^{*}(P)\supseteq FT^{*}(Q)
.

The orientation of the symbol
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
aligns with that of the safety preorder,
discussed in the next section.
The following proposition says that the preorder
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
is a
precongruence
for the
operators of CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
, or that recursion-free CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
-contexts are
monotone
w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
.

Theorem 7.2
.

fft precongruence
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a unary recursion-free CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
-context.

If
P\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q
then

\mathcal{C}(P)\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}\mathcal{C}(Q)
.

Proof 7.3
.

Let
G:=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha.P_{i}
. In case
\alpha_{i}\neq\tau
for all
i\in I
, then
(
10
)–(
6.16
) imply (or generalise to)

FT^{*}(G)=\textit{ini}_{I(G)}\!\left(\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\raisebox{5.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle i\in I$}\\[-11.0pt]
\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\in A\end{array}}\{\alpha_{i}\sigma\mid\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P_{i})\}\cup\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\raisebox{5.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle i\in I$}\\[-11.0pt]
\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}={\rm t}\end{array}}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\{X\sigma\mid X\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P_{i})\wedge\mathcal{I}(G)\cap X=\emptyset\}\cup\mbox{}\\
\left\{Xa\sigma\left|\,a\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P_{i})\begin{array}[]{@{}l}\mbox{}\wedge\mathcal{I}(G)\cap X=\emptyset\\
\mbox{}\wedge a\mathbin{\in}X\end{array}\right\}\right.\end{array}\right)\!\right)\!.

Here
\mathcal{I}(G):=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}\setminus\{{\rm t}\}
.
Moreover, if
\alpha_{i}=\tau
for at least one
i\in I
, then

FT^{*}(G)=\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\raisebox{5.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle i\in I$}\\[-11.0pt]
\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\in A\end{array}}\{\alpha_{i}\sigma\mid\sigma\in FT^{*}(P_{i})\}\cup\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\raisebox{5.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle i\in I$}\\[-11.0pt]
\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}=\tau\end{array}}FT^{*}(P_{i})\;.

There equations immediately imply the monotonicity of the guarded choice operators w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
.
The monotonicity of
\mathbin{\|_{S}}
,
\tau_{I}
and
\mathcal{R}
follows from Properties (
4
),
(
8
) and (
9
).

Definition 7.4
.

rfftpre
Write
P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q
, for
P,Q\mathbin{\in}\mbox{\bbb P}
, iff
FT^{r*}(P)\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q)
.

In this definition, the
poststab
bit of Definition
plays a crucial rôle. Without it, we
would have
b\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}\tau.b
, since
FT^{r*}(b)=FT^{r*}(\tau.b)\uplus\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\}
. This would cause a
failure of monotonicity, as
a+b\not\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}a+\tau.b
, for only the latter process has a partial
failure trace
\{a\}\top
. With the
poststab
bit one obtains

b\not\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}\tau.b
, since
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in FT^{r*}(\tau.b)
, yet
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\notin FT^{r*}(b)
.
The preorder
\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}
still relates processes of which only one is stable;
for instance
b\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$/$}}}{{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[1.29167pt]{\tiny$\backslash$}}}\!\!X|X=b+\tau.X\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\backslash$}}}{{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}[1.29167pt]{\tiny$/$}}}$}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

initials equal
If
P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q
(or
P\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q
) and
P
and
Q
are both stable, then
\mathcal{I}(P)=\mathcal{I}(Q)
.

Proof 7.6
.

If
a\in\mathcal{I}(Q)
then
a\top\in FT^{r*}(Q)\subseteq FT^{r*}(P)
, so
a\mathbin{\in}\mathcal{I}(P)
.

Moreover, if
a\mathbin{\notin}\mathcal{I}(Q)
then
\{a\}\top\mathbin{\in}FT^{r*}(Q)\subseteq FT^{r*}(P)
, so
a\mathbin{\notin}\mathcal{I}(P)
.

Theorem 7.7
.

rfft precongruence
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a unary recursion-free CCSP
t
-context.

If
P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q
then

\mathcal{C}(P)\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}\mathcal{C}(Q)
.

Proof 7.8
.

The monotonicity of
\mathbin{\|_{S}}
,
\tau_{I}
and
\mathcal{R}
follows from Properties
(
18
)–(
23
), using Lemma
.
The monotonicity of
\alpha.\_\!\_\,
follows from (
10
)–(
6.16
),
using that
FT^{r*}(P)\mathbin{\supseteq}FT^{r*}(Q)
implies
FT^{*}(P)\mathbin{\supseteq}FT^{*}(Q)
.

The monotonicity of
+
can be formulated as
P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q\Rightarrow P+R\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q+R

(the requirement
R+P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}R+Q
then follows by symmetry).
So let
P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q
, i.e.,
FT^{r*}(P)\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q)
.

•

Let
\neg{\it stable}(P)
and
\neg{\it stable}(R)
. Then
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\notin FT^{r*}(P)\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q)
, so
\neg{\it stable}(Q)
.
Hence

FT^{r*}(P+R)=FT^{r*}(P)\cup FT^{r*}(R)\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q)\cup FT^{r*}(R)=FT^{r*}(Q+R).\vskip-4.30554pt

•

The case
\neg{\it stable}(P)
and
{\it stable}(R)
proceeds likewise.

In all remaining cases I assume
{\it stable}(P)
.
Note that
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\notin FT^{r*}(P)\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q)
.

•

Let
{\it stable}(Q)
and
\neg{\it stable}(R)
. Then

FT^{r*}(P+R)=\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\}\cup FT^{r*}(R)\linebreak\supseteq\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{r*}(Q)\}\cup FT^{r*}(R)=FT^{r*}(Q+R).

•

Let
\neg{\it stable}(Q)
and
\neg{\it stable}(R)
. Now all
\sigma\in FT^{r*}(Q)
must have the form
\top
or
a\zeta
.
Namely if
X\zeta\in FT^{r*}(Q)
then one would have
\mbox{\scriptsize\sc poststab}\in FT^{r*}(Q)
.
Hence

FT^{r*}(P+R)=\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\}\cup FT^{r*}(R)\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q)\cup FT^{r*}(R)=FT^{r*}(Q+R)
.

•

Let
{\it stable}(Q)
and
{\it stable}(R)
. Then
\mathcal{I}(P)=\mathcal{I}(Q)
by Lemma
.
Using this, the desired monotonicity property follows from the fourth case of (
6.16
).

•

Let
\neg{\it stable}(Q)
and
{\it stable}(R)
. Again all
\sigma\in FT^{r*}(Q)
must have the form
\top
or
a\zeta
.
Let
H
and
K
be as in (
6.16
), but with
R
substituted for
Q
.
Now

\displaystyle FT^{r*}(P+R)

\displaystyle=\textit{ini}_{\mathcal{I}(P){\cup}\mathcal{I}(Q)}\big(H\big){\cup}\{\mbox{\scriptsize\sc stab}\}{\cup}K\supseteq H{\cup}\{\top\}

\displaystyle\supseteq FT^{r*}(Q){\cup}\{a\sigma\mid a\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{r*}(R)\}=FT^{r*}(Q{+}R).

Theorem 7.9
.

full abstraction preorder

\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}
is the largest precongruence for the operators of CCSP
t

that is included in
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
.

Proof 7.10
.

Theorem
shows that
\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}
is a precongruence for the operators of CCSP
t
.
By Definition
,
\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}
is included in
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
,
i.e.,
P\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q
implies
P\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q
.
Thus it suffices to find, for any two processes
P
and
Q
with
P\not\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}Q
, a CCSP
t
-context

\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{C}(P)\not\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}\mathcal{C}(Q)
.
This proceeds exactly as in the proof of Theorem
. Of course the symmetric case “
\sigma\in FT^{r*}(P)\setminus FT^{r*}(Q)
”
is skipped, as it is not needed here.

8.
The coarsest precongruence respecting safety properties

In
[
Gla10
]
I proposed a way to define refinement preorders
\sqsubseteq
on processes,
where
P\sqsubseteq Q
says that for all practical purposes under consideration,
Q
is at least as
suitable as
P
, i.e., it will never harm to replace
P
by
Q
. In the stepwise design of systems,

P
may be closer to a specification, and
Q
to an implementation. Each such a refinement preorder also
yields a semantic equivalence
\equiv
, with
P\equiv Q
saying that for practical purposes
P
and

Q
are equally suitable; i.e., one can be replaced by the other without untoward side effects.
Naturally,
P\equiv Q
iff both
P\sqsubseteq Q
and
Q\sqsubseteq P
.

The method of
[
Gla10
]
equips the choice of
\sqsubseteq
with two parameters:
a class
\fG
of good properties of processes, ones that may be required of processes in a given
range of applications, and a class
\fO
of useful operators for combining processes.
The first requirement on
\sqsubseteq
is

P\sqsubseteq Q\Rightarrow\forall\varphi\in\fG.\;(P\models\varphi\Rightarrow Q\models\varphi)

where
P\models\varphi
denotes that process
P
has the property
\varphi
.
If this holds,
\sqsubseteq

respects
or
preserves
the properties in
\fG
.
The second requirement is that
\sqsubseteq
is a
precongruence
for
\fO
:

P\sqsubseteq Q\Rightarrow\mathcal{C}[P]\sqsubseteq\mathcal{C}[Q]
for any context
\mathcal{C}[\_\!\_\,]
built from
operators from
\fO
. Given the choice of
\fG
and
\fO
, the preorder recommended by
[
Gla10
]

is the
coarsest
, or largest, one satisfying both requirements.
This preorder is called
fully abstract
w.r.t.
\fG
and
\fO
, and
is characterised by

P\sqsubseteq Q~~\Leftrightarrow~~\forall\fO\mbox{-context }\mathcal{C}[\_\!\_].\;\forall\varphi\in\fG.\;(\mathcal{C}[P]\models\varphi\Rightarrow\mathcal{C}[Q]\models\varphi).

The corresponding semantic equivalence identifies processes only when this is enforced by the two
requirements above.

Naturally, increasing the class
\fO
of operators makes the resulting fully abstract preorder

finer
, or smaller. The same holds when increasing the class
\fG
of properties.
In
[
Gla10
]
I employed a class
\fO
of operators that was effectively equivalent to the
operators of CCSP
g
. Here I use the operators of CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
, thus adding time-outs.
In this section, following
[
Gla10
, Section 3]
, I take as class
\fG
of good properties the

safety properties
, saying that something bad will never happen.
What exactly counts as a safety property could be open to some debate.
However, the
canonical safety property
, proposed in
[
Gla10
]
, is definitely in this class.
I first characterise the preorder
\sqsubseteq_{\it safety}
that is fully abstract w.r.t. the
operators of CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
and this single safety property. It turns out to be
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
.
Then I argue that adding any other safety properties to the class
\fG
could not possible
make the resulting preorder any finer.
An immediately corollary of this and Theorem
is that
\sqsubseteq^{r*}_{FT}

is fully abstract for the operators of CCSP
t
and the class of safety properties.

Full abstraction w.r.t. the canonical safety property

To formulate the canonical safety property, assume that the alphabet
A

of visible actions contains one specific action
b
, whose occurrence
is
bad
. The property now says that
b
will never happen
.

{defi}

A process
P
satisfies the
canonical safety property
, notation

P\models\textit{safety}(b)
, if no partial failure trace of
P
contains the action
b
.

Lemma 8.1
.

initial
If
a\eta\in FT^{*}(P)
then
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle a$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
or
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
.

Proof 8.2
.

That
a\eta\in FT^{*}(P)
can be derived only from the second or third rule of Table
3
.

Corollary 8.3
.

initial
If
Xa\eta\in FT^{*}(P)
is obtained from the fourth rule of Table
3
, then
a\notin X
.

Proof 8.4
.

The fourth rule requires that
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\}
, and
that
a\eta\in FT^{*}(P)
. The latter implies
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle a$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
or
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
by Lemma
.
So
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle a$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
and
a\notin X
.

As encountered already in the proof of Theorem
, I call an action
b

fresh

for a process
P
if for each process
R
reachable from
P
one has
R{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle b$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
.
In that case
P\models\textit{safety}(b)
.
I call it
fresh
for a partial failure trace
\sigma\in(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top
if
b
does not occur in

\sigma
, either as action or inside a refusal set in
\sigma
.

Theorem 8.5
.

may
For each
\sigma\in(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top
for which
b
is fresh, there exists a CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
process
T_{\sigma}

such that

\tau_{A\setminus\{b\}}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{A\setminus\{b\}}}P)\not\models\textit{safety}(b)~~\Leftrightarrow~~\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)

for each CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
process
P
for which
b
is fresh.

Proof 8.6
.

Let
B:=A{\setminus}\{b\}
, the set of all visible actions except
b
.
Define
T_{\sigma}
with structural induction on
\sigma
:

T_{\top}:={\rm t}.b

T_{c\rho}:=\tau+c.T_{\rho}

T_{X\eta}:={\rm t}.T_{\eta}+\sum_{a\in X}a

T_{Xd\rho}:={\rm t}.(d.T_{\rho}+\sum_{a\in X\setminus\{d\}}a)+\sum_{a\in X}a

where,
c,d\in B
,
X\subseteq B
,
d\in X
, and
\eta
is not of the form
d\rho
with
d\mathbin{\in}X
.
Note that
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\not\models\textit{safety}(b)
iff there is a path

\pi:=\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}Q_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.21384pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}Q_{\ell}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle b$\>}\hfil}}$}}Q^{\prime}
,
and this path gives rise to a partial failure trace
\beta b\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))

by application of the rules of Table
3
. Due to the operator
\tau_{B}
, each of the
\alpha_{i}

must be
\tau
or
{\rm t}
.

To obtain “
\Rightarrow
” I assume
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\not\models\textit{safety}(b)

and apply structural induction on
\sigma
.

•

Let
\sigma=\top
. Then
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
for all
P
.

•

Let
\sigma=c\rho
.
For the process
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)
to ever reach a
b
-transition, the component

T_{\sigma}=\tau+c.T_{\rho}
must take the
c
-transition to
T_{\rho}
.
So the beginning of
\pi
must have the form

\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.21384pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})

for some
n\geq 0
, where
\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})\not\models\textit{safety}(b)
.
Let
P_{0}:=P
.
All of these
\alpha_{i}
must be
\tau
, for
{\rm t}
-transitions lose out from the outgoing

\tau
-transition from
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{i-1})
. Technically, if
\alpha_{i}={\rm t}
,
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
then the fifth and sixth rules of Table
3
do not allow any partial failure trace of

\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{i})
to give rise to a partial failure trace of
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{i-1})
.
The above path must stem from a path

\qquad T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta_{n}$\>}\hfil}}$}}T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle c$\>}\hfil}}$}}T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime}

that in turns stems from a path

P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\beta_{n}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle c$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}
.
All of these
\beta_{i}
must be
\tau
, as the synchronisation on
B
stops all visible actions
stemming from
P
.

By induction
\rho\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
.
The second and third rules of Table
3
yield
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
.

•

Let
\sigma=X\eta
, where
X\subseteq B
and
\eta
is not of the form
d\rho
with
d\mathbin{\in}X
.
For the process
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)
to ever reach a
b
-transition, the component

T_{\sigma}={\rm t}.T_{\eta}+\sum_{a\in X}a
must take the
{\rm t}
-transition to
T_{\eta}
.
So the beginning of
\pi
must have the form

\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.21384pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\eta}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})

for some
n\geq 0
, where
\tau_{B}(T_{\eta}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})\not\models\textit{safety}(b)
.
By induction
\eta\in FT^{*}(P_{n})
.
As above, again using that visible actions from
P
are blocked by
\mathbin{\|_{B}}
,
it must be that
P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.21384pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}P_{n}
.
I proceed with induction on
n
.

Base case:
It must be that
P=P_{n}{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\}
, for
otherwise
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})
would have an outgoing
\tau
-transition that wins
from the
{\rm t}
-transition to
\tau_{B}(T_{\eta}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})
. The fourth rule of Table
3
yields
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
.

Induction step:
By induction
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P_{1})
. In case
\alpha_{1}=\tau
one obtains

\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
by the third rule of Table
3
. In case
\alpha_{1}={\rm t}
, it must be that

P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\}
, for otherwise
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)

would have an outgoing
\tau
-transition that wins from the
{\rm t}
-transition to
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{1})
.
The fifth rule of Table
3
yields
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
.

•

Let
\sigma\mathbin{=}Xd\rho
with
d\mathbin{\in}X
. Writing
R
for
d.T_{\rho}+\sum_{a\in X\setminus\{d\}}a
,
the first part of
\pi
must have the form

\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.21384pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})

\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to23.75565pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n+1}$\>}}$}}\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n+1})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to23.75565pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n+2}$\>}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to16.94998pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{m}$\>}}$}}\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{m})\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})

for some
m\geq n\geq 0
, where
\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})\not\models\textit{safety}(b)
.
By induction
\rho\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
.
As above, it must be that

P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{2}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.21384pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n}$\>}}$}}P_{n}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to23.75565pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n+1}$\>}}$}}P_{n+1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to23.75565pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{n+2}$\>}}$}}\cdots\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to16.94998pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha_{m}$\>}}$}}P_{m}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle d$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}
.
Necessarily,
P_{n}{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\}
, for otherwise
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})

would have an outgoing
\tau
-transition that wins from the
{\rm t}
-transition to
\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P_{n})
.
In particular,
P_{n}{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle d$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
, whereas
P_{m}{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle d$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
, so
n\neq m
.
Moreover,
\alpha_{n+1}={\rm t}
, for
\alpha_{n+1}=\tau
contradicts with
P_{n}{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
.
Let
k\leq m
be the highest index such that
\alpha_{k}={\rm t}
.
Then
d\rho\in FT^{*}(P_{k})
, by the second and third rules of Table
3
.
For each index
i
with
\alpha_{i}={\rm t}
one must have
P_{i-1}{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\}
,
for the same reason as above. The sixth rule of Table
3
yields
Xd\rho\in FT^{*}(P_{k-1})
.
Successive applications of the third and fifth rules yield
Xd\rho\in FT^{*}(P_{j})
for all
j<k
,
so in particular
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
.

To obtain “
\Leftarrow
”, I will show that

\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
implies
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.
Here
A
is the set of all visible actions.
In doing so, I apply induction on the derivation of
\sigma\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
.

Suppose
\sigma\mathbin{=}\top\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
is derived from the first rule of Table
3
.
Since
T_{\sigma}={\rm t}.b.0
one has

\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(b.0\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle b$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(0\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)
.
Hence
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
, using the first, second and sixth rules of Table
3
,
and using that
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P){\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in A\cup\{\tau\}
.

Suppose
\sigma=c\rho\in FT^{*}(P)
is derived from the second rule of Table
3
.
Then
P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle c$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}
and
\rho\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
.
By induction,
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime}))
.
Since
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})
,
it follows with the third rule of Table
3

that
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.

Suppose
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P)
is derived from the third rule of Table
3
.
Then
P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}
and
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
.
By induction,
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime}))
.
Since
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})
,
it follows with the third rule of Table
3
that
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.

Suppose
\sigma\mathbin{=}X\eta\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
is derived from the fourth rule of Table
3
.
Then
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\mathbin{\in}X\cup\{\tau\}
and
\eta\in FT^{*}(P)
.
In the special case that
\eta=d\rho
, by
d\notin X
.
By induction
Ab\top\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\eta}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.
Since
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\eta}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)
,
and
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P){\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in A\cup\{\tau\}
,
the fifth rule of Table
3
yields
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.

Suppose
\sigma\mathbin{=}X\eta\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
is derived from the fifth rule of Table
3
.
Then
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\mathbin{\in}X\cup\{\tau\}
,

P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}
and
\sigma\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
.
So
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P){\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in A\cup\{\tau\}
,
and
Ab\top\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime}))
by induction.
Since
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})
,
the fifth rule yields
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.

Suppose
\sigma\mathbin{=}Xd\rho\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
is derived from the sixth rule of Table
3
.
Then
P{\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\mathbin{\in}X\cup\{\tau\}
,
d\mathbin{\in}X
,

P\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}P^{\prime}
and
d\rho\in FT^{*}(P^{\prime})
. So
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P){\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}

and
\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P){\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{$\mathord{-}\mkern-6.0mu\leaders{\hbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-6.0mu\mathord{\not}\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{\rightarrow}$}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\alpha$\>}\hfil}}$}}}
for all
\alpha\in A\cup\{\tau\}
,
where
R:=d.T_{\rho}+\sum_{a\in X\setminus\{d\}}a
.

By induction
Ab\top\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{d\rho}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime}))
.
As
\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P)\linebreak\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.33325pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.33325pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}\tau_{B}(R\mathbin{\|_{B}}P^{\prime})
,
two applications of the fifth rule of Table
3
yield
Ab\top\in FT^{*}(\tau_{B}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{B}}P))
.

Corollary 8.7
.

safety

\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
is fully abstract w.r.t. the operators of CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
and the canonical safety property.

Proof 8.8
.

By Theorem
,
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
is a precongruence for the operators of CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
.
By definition,
P\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q
implies
P\models\textit{safety}(b)\Rightarrow Q\models\textit{safety}(b)
.
It remains to show that
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
is the coarsest preorder with these properties.
To this end it suffices to find for any processes
P
and
Q
with
P\not\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q

a recursion-free CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
-context
\mathcal{C}[\_\!\_\,]
such that
\mathcal{C}[P]\models\textit{safety}(b)
,
yet
\mathcal{C}[Q]\not\models\textit{safety}(b)
.

So assume
P\not\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}Q
.
By applying a bijective renaming operator on
P
and
Q
, mapping
A
to
A\setminus\{b\}
,
I may assume that
b
is fresh for
P
and
Q
.
Take
\sigma\in FT^{*}(Q)\setminus FT^{*}(P)
. By Property (*) in the proof of Theorem

I may assume that
b
is fresh for
\sigma
. Now Theorem
yields

\tau_{A\setminus\{b\}}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{A\setminus\{b\}}}P)\models\textit{safety}(b)
, yet

\tau_{A\setminus\{b\}}(T_{\sigma}\mathbin{\|_{A\setminus\{b\}}}Q)\not\models\textit{safety}(b)
.

Full abstraction w.r.t. general safety properties

The above says that the unique preorder
\sqsubseteq_{\it safety}
that is fully abstract w.r.t. the
operators of CCSP
{}^{g}_{\rm t}
and the canonical safety property coincides with
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
.
Without the time-out operator,
\sqsubseteq_{\it safety}
would be way less discriminating, and coincides
with reverse weak partial trace inclusion
[
Gla10
]
.
I now check whether one could get an even finer preorder than
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
by also considering other safety properties.

To arrive at a general concept of safety property for LTSs, the paper
[
Gla10
]
assumes that some
notion of
bad
is defined. This induces the safety property saying that this bad thing will
never happen. To judge whether a process
P
satisfies this safety property, one should judge
whether
P
can reach a state in which one would say that this bad thing had happened. But all
observable behaviour of
P
that is recorded in an LTS until one comes to such a verdict, is the
sequence of visible actions performed until that point. Thus the safety property is completely
determined by the set sequences of visible actions that, when performed by
P
, lead to such a
judgement. Therefore,
[
Gla10
]
proposes to define the concept of a safety property in terms of such a
set: a safety property is given by a set
B\subseteq A^{*}
of
bad
partial traces.
A process
P

satisfies
this property, notation
P\models\textit{safety}(B)
, if
P
has no
partial trace in
B
. It follows immediately from this definition that partial trace equivalent
processes satisfy the same safety properties of this kind.

The present paper allows for a stronger concept of safety property. Here the observable behaviour of
a process that is recorded until one comes to the verdict that something bad has happened can be modelled
as a partial failure trace. Based on this, my definition is analogous to the one in
[
Gla10
]
:

Definition 8.9
.

safety
A
safety property
of CCSP
t
processes is given by a set
B\subseteq(A\cup\Pow(A))^{*}\top

of
bad
partial failure traces. A process
P

satisfies
this property, notation
P\models\textit{safety}(B)
,
if
FT^{*}(P)\cap B=\emptyset
.

It follows immediately that
\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}
respects all safety properties.
Consequently, as in
[
Gla10
]
, for the definition of
\sqsubseteq_{\it safety}
it does not matter
whether all safety properties are considered, or only the canonical one.

Trace equivalence and its congruence closure

A possible definition of a (weak) partial trace is simply a partial failure trace from which
all refusal sets have been omitted. Define two processes to be (weak) (partial) trace equivalent iff
they have the same partial traces. Now implies that
\equiv_{FT}^{*}
is the congruence closure
of trace equivalence, that is, the coarsest congruence for the operators of CCSP
t
that is finer
than trace equivalence.

May testing

May testing
was proposed by De Nicola & Hennessy in
[
DH84
]
for the process algebra CCS.
Translated to CCSP
t
it works as follows. A
test
is a CCSP
t
process that instead of
visible actions from the alphabet
A
, uses visible actions from the alphabet
A\uplus\{\omega\}
,
where
\omega
is a fresh action reporting “success”. A process
P

may pass
a test
T

iff
\tau_{A}(T\mathbin{\|_{A}}P)
has some partial failure trace
\beta\omega\top
, or, equivalently,
has a partial trace containing the action
\omega
. A process with such a trace represents a system
with an execution that eventually reports success. Now define the preorder

\sqsubseteq_{\it may}
on CCSP
t
processes by

P\sqsubseteq_{\it may}Q
iff each test
T
that
P
may pass, may also be passed by
Q
.

May testing contrasts with
must testing
, which requires
that
each
execution leads to a state that can report success.

Theorem 8.10
.

may2

P\sqsubseteq_{\it may}Q
iff
Q\sqsubseteq^{*}_{FT}P
.

Proof 8.11
.

Note that
R
has some partial failure trace
\beta\omega\top
iff
R\not\models\textit{safety}(\omega)
.
Using this, Theorem
is an immediately consequence of Theorem
, with
\omega
in the rôle of
b
.
The side conditions of
b
being fresh in Theorem
are redundant in this context, as
\omega

is fresh by construction.

So the may-preorder is the converse of the safety preorder. This is related to calling
\omega
a
success action, instead of
b
a bad action. The may-preorder preserves the property that a process

can
do something good, whereas the safety preorder preserves the property that a process

can not
so something bad.

9.
Conclusion

This paper extended the model of labelled transition systems with time-out transitions
s_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle{\rm t}$\>}\hfil}}$}}s_{2}
.
Such a transition is assumed to be instantaneous and unobservable by the environment, just like a
transition
s_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.00002pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox to15.00002pt{\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=6.45831pt,depth=3.44444pt,width=0.0pt\>$\scriptstyle\tau$\>}\hfil}}$}}s_{2}
, but becomes available only a positive but finite amount of time
after the represented system reaches state
s_{1}
. This extension allows the implementation of a
simple priority mechanism, thereby increasing the expressiveness of the model.

To denote such labelled transition systems I extended the standard process algebra CCSP with the action
prefixing operator
{\rm t}.\_\!\_\,
. Many semantic preorders
\sqsubseteq
have been defined on
labelled transition systems, and thereby on the expressions in CCSP and other process algebras.

P\sqsubseteq Q
says that process
Q
is a
refinement
of process
P
, where
P
is closer to the
specification of a system, and
Q
to its implementation.
The least one may wish to require from such a preorder is

(1)

that it is a precongruence for the
static
operators of CCSP: partially synchronous parallel
composition, renaming, and abstraction by renaming visible actions into the hidden action

\tau
,

(2)

and that it respects (basic) safety properties, meaning that any safety property of a
process
P
also holds for its refinements
Q
.

The coarsest preorder with these properties is well known to be the reverse weak partial trace inclusion
(see
e.g.

[
Gla10
]
). Accordingly, weak partial trace inclusion is the preorder generated by

may testing

[
DH84
]
.

After the extension with time-out transitions, the weak partial trace preorder is no longer a
precongruence for the static operators of CCSP. I here characterised the coarsest preorder satisfying
(1) and (2) above as the
partial failure trace
preorder.
Again, its converse is the preorder generated by may testing.
All the above also applies to semantic equivalences, arising as the kernels of these preorders.

To obtain a (pre)congruence for all CCSP operators, including the CCS choice
+
, one needs to use
a
rooted
version of the partial failure trace preorder, as is common in the study of preorders
that abstract from the hidden action
\tau
.

The work reported here remains in the realm of untimed process algebra, in the sense that the
progress of time is not quantified. In the study of Timed CSP
[
RR87
,
DS95
,
Sch95
,
RR99
]
,
similar work has been done in a setting where the progress of time is quantified.
Also there a form of failure trace semantics was found to be the right equivalence, and the
connection with may testing was made in
[
Sch95
]
. Since the bookkeeping of time in

[
RR87
,
DS95
,
Sch95
,
RR99
]
is strongly interwoven with the formalisation of failure traces, it is not
easy to determine whether my semantics can in some way be seen as a abstraction of the one for Timed
CCSP, for instance by instantiating each quantified passage of time with a nondeterministic choice allowing

any
amount of time. This appears to be a question worthy of further research.
A contribution of the present work is that the transformation of a failures based semantics of CSP

[
BHR84
,
Hoa85
]
to a failure trace based semantics of Timed CSP
[
RR87
,
DS95
,
Sch95
,
RR99
]

is not really necessitated by quantification of time—often seen as the main difference
between CSP and Timed CSP—but rather by the introduction of a time-out operator (quantified or not).

Future work includes proving a congruence result for recursion, finding complete axiomatisations,
and extending the approach from partial to complete failure traces, so that liveness properties will
be respected. In that setting the expressiveness questions of Section
1.4
can be studied.
The adaption of strong bisimilarity to the setting with time-out transitions is studied in
[
Gla20
]
.

Appendix A
Proofs of congruence properties

This appendix contains the proofs of the explicit characterisations of the operators
\mathbin{\|_{S}}
,

\tau_{I}
and
\mathcal{R}
on (rooted) partial failure trace sets: Properties (

),
(
8
), (
9

) and (

These are the central parts of the proofs showing that

Proposition A.1
.

.

.

\sigma_{\rm L}\mathbin{:=}a\rho_{\rm L}\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)

\sigma_{\rm R}\mathbin{:=}a\rho_{\rm R}\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(Q)
.
Also this decomposition is valid.
In case (iii)
\sigma_{\rm L}\mathbin{:=}\rho_{\rm L}\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(P)
and
\sigma_{\rm R}\mathbin{:=}a\rho_{\rm R}\mathbin{\in}FT^{*}(Q)
.
Also this decomposition is valid.

.
